# KITABU QUEST Grade 8 English

## Title Page

KITABU QUEST Grade 8 English

Kenya CBC content draft for review

Mascot: fox rabbit

This content-first draft was regenerated from Kitabu curriculum data using a topic-by-topic book plan. It is designed to help learners read, practise, discuss, create, and revise with confidence.

Cover artwork is intentionally deferred until all content has passed review and is marked ready-for-cover.

## How To Use This Book

This book helps Grade 8 learners study English through clear lessons, worked examples, activities, practice, and revision.

Use the inquiry questions to think first. Read the explanation. Try the guided example. Complete the activity. Answer the practice questions. Correct your work and ask for support where needed.

Read, speak, listen, write, and edit in connected language tasks.

Every unit has a local example, a misconception to avoid, success criteria, and a check for understanding.

## Learning Skills In This Book

Each chapter builds knowledge, skills, values, creativity, communication, collaboration, critical thinking, digital literacy, and self-confidence.

Look for these page types: Learn, Example, Activity, Practice, Reflection, Home Link, and Review.

How to study well: read the goal first, underline new words, try the example before reading the answer, discuss with a partner, and correct your work after feedback. Do not skip the reflection questions. They help you notice what you understand and what needs more practice.

## Polite Language: Telephone Etiquette: Lesson Opener

Learning area: Polite Language: Telephone Etiquette

Start here: a class discussion, reading club, letter, report, speech, or story situation linked to Polite Language: Telephone Etiquette.

Curriculum link: This lesson follows the Grade 8 curriculum sequence and focuses on Polite Language: Telephone Etiquette.

By the end of this lesson sequence, you should be able to:
- identify polite words and phrases in telephone conversations
- conduct a telephone conversation using polite words and expressions
- acknowledge the significance of etiquette in telephone conversations.

Inquiry questions:
- Why should one be polite when speaking over telephone?
- How do we ensure politeness in a telephone conversation?

Before reading, write one thing you already know and one question you want this lesson to answer.

## Polite Language: Telephone Etiquette: Learn And Example

Think of the listener or reader first. The task is connected to Polite Language: Telephone Etiquette.

Important idea: Polite Language: Telephone Etiquette.

Speaking and listening are active skills. Listen for the main point, take brief notes, respond respectfully, and support your own point with a reason or example. Clear speech uses pace, volume, eye contact, and appropriate tone.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

Success criteria:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

For Grade 8, use clear sentences, examples, and respectful discussion.

Inline visual support:

| Communication part | What to show |
| --- | --- |
| Purpose | The reading, listening, speaking, writing, or grammar task in Polite Language: Telephone Etiquette. |
| Audience | Who needs to understand the message. |
| Evidence or example | A detail from the passage, conversation, notice, report, or spoken task. |
| Revision | One sentence improved for clarity, tone, spelling, punctuation, or grammar. |

Polite language model for Polite Language: Telephone Etiquette: Match words to the setting and listener.

Situation: A learner is helping visitors during a school hospitality day. One visitor wants to reserve a table near a quiet corner.

Weak response: Sit there. I do not know if the table is free.

Improved response: Good afternoon. Let me check whether the quiet-corner table is available. If it has already been reserved, I can offer you another table near the window.

Why it works:
1. It opens politely.
2. It explains the action instead of sounding dismissive.
3. It gives an alternative.
4. It fits a hotel, dining, or public-service setting.

Your turn: Write a polite request, apology, or reservation response linked to polite language: telephone etiquette, then underline the words that make the tone respectful.

Key words:
- Polite Language: Telephone Etiquette
- Polite
- Language
- Telephone
- Etiquette

## Polite Language: Telephone Etiquette: Activity And Practice

Activity for Polite Language: Telephone Etiquette: Complete the listening, reading, grammar, writing, or speaking task named in the outcomes below, then improve one answer after feedback.

Use this routine for every answer: identify the exact language skill, state the audience or purpose, use evidence or an example, organise the response, and edit one sentence.

1. Prepare a one-minute spoken response with one point, one reason, and one example, then ask a partner for feedback on clarity and tone. Target outcome: identify polite words and phrases in telephone conversations.
2. Prepare a one-minute spoken response with one point, one reason, and one example, then ask a partner for feedback on clarity and tone. Target outcome: conduct a telephone conversation using polite words and expressions.
3. Prepare a one-minute spoken response with one point, one reason, and one example, then ask a partner for feedback on clarity and tone. Target outcome: acknowledge the significance of etiquette in telephone conversations.

Correction check:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

Reflection: What meaning became clearer after editing? Which sentence still needs work?

Home link: Ask someone at home for a short story, instruction, opinion, or example linked to polite language: telephone etiquette. Write four clear sentences about it.

## Reading 1.2.1Extensive: Learn And Example

Before answering, decide whether you are reading, speaking, listening, writing, or editing.

Important idea: Reading 1.2.1Extensive.

Reading well means tracking meaning, not just finishing words. Identify the main idea, notice how details support it, and answer with evidence from the text. For literature, connect character, setting, plot, language, and theme.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

Success criteria:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

For Grade 8, use clear sentences, examples, and respectful discussion.

Inline visual support:

| Communication part | What to show |
| --- | --- |
| Purpose | The reading, listening, speaking, writing, or grammar task in Reading 1.2.1Extensive. |
| Audience | Who needs to understand the message. |
| Evidence or example | A detail from the passage, conversation, notice, report, or spoken task. |
| Revision | One sentence improved for clarity, tone, spelling, punctuation, or grammar. |

Reading model for Reading 1.2.1Extensive: Read the short passage, then answer with evidence.

Passage: Amina borrowed a library book about a school clean-water campaign. She first skimmed the headings, then read the middle paragraph slowly because it explained why learners labelled taps, reported leaks, and kept drinking cups clean. In her notebook, she wrote one main idea and two details from the paragraph instead of guessing from the title alone.

Question: What does Amina's behaviour show about careful reading?

Model answer:
1. Point: Amina reads carefully and uses evidence.
2. Evidence: She skims headings, rereads the paragraph, and records one main idea with two details.
3. Explanation: These actions show that a strong reading answer comes from the text, not from a quick guess.

Grade 8 check: A strong answer names the idea, quotes or paraphrases evidence, and explains why the evidence matters.

Key words:
- Reading 1.2.1Extensive
- Reading
- 1.2.1Extensive

## Word classes: Compound Nouns: Learn And Example

Begin with a real language task: a class discussion, reading club, letter, report, speech, or story situation linked to Word classes: Compound Nouns.

Important idea: Word classes: Compound Nouns.

Grammar helps meaning become clear. Do not memorise a rule alone; test it inside a real sentence. Check who or what the sentence is about, what action or state is shown, and how punctuation guides the reader.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

Success criteria:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

For Grade 8, use clear sentences, examples, and respectful discussion.

Inline visual support:

| Communication part | What to show |
| --- | --- |
| Purpose | The reading, listening, speaking, writing, or grammar task in Word classes: Compound Nouns. |
| Audience | Who needs to understand the message. |
| Evidence or example | A detail from the passage, conversation, notice, report, or spoken task. |
| Revision | One sentence improved for clarity, tone, spelling, punctuation, or grammar. |

Language model for Word classes: Compound Nouns: Join ideas without making the sentence confusing.

Original sentence: The class read the passage. The passage explained word classes: compound nouns. The group needed evidence.

Revision steps:
1. Keep the main idea clear.
2. Join related ideas with because, when, although, or which only where the meaning fits.
3. Remove repeated words.
4. Improved sentence: When the class read the passage about word classes: compound nouns, the group underlined evidence before answering.

Explanation: A useful complex sentence links ideas and still keeps the main action easy to find.

Your turn: Combine two short sentences about word classes: compound nouns into one clear sentence.

Key words:

Think of the listener or reader first. The task is connected to Types of Sentences: Interrogative sentences.

Language model for Types of Sentences: Interrogative sentences: Improve a sentence so the meaning is precise.

Draft sentence: The learner wrote clear examples, but the explanation need one reason.

Revision steps:
1. Find the subject of the second clause: the explanation.
2. Check the verb form: explanation is singular, so use needs.
3. Keep the correction small so the meaning stays the same.
4. Improved sentence: The learner wrote clear examples, but the explanation needs one reason.

Explanation: Correct agreement and exact word order help the reader understand the idea without guessing.

Your turn: Write one correct sentence about types of sentences: interrogative sentences, then underline the subject and verb.

Key words:
- Types of Sentences: Interrogative sentences

## Writing 1.5.1 Writing: Learn And Example

Think of the listener or reader first. The task is connected to Writing 1.5.1 Writing.

Important idea: Writing 1.5.1 Writing.

Writing improves when purpose, audience, structure, and editing work together. Plan the main idea first, arrange details in a sensible order, choose exact words, and revise at least one sentence after feedback.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

Success criteria:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

For Grade 8, use clear sentences, examples, and respectful discussion.

Writing model for Writing 1.5.1 Writing: Plan before writing.

Task: Write a short response that explains a learning activity connected to this topic.

Plan:
1. Purpose: explain what happened and what was learned.
2. Audience: classmates, teacher, parents, or public readers.
3. Opening sentence: Our group studied writing 1.5.1 writing by reading the task, discussing evidence, and improving one draft.
4. Three details: what learners did, what evidence was used, and what changed after feedback.
5. Closing sentence: The activity showed that clear planning and revision improve communication.

Model paragraph: Our group studied writing 1.5.1 writing by reading the task, discussing evidence, and improving one draft. Each learner contributed one sentence, then the group checked word choice and punctuation. We revised the weakest sentence so the message became clearer. The activity showed that clear planning and revision improve communication.

Your turn: Use the same plan for a paragraph, report, letter, or speech linked to this topic.

Listening model for Reading 2.2.1 Intensive: persona in a given poem: Catch key details from a recording or teacher-read text.

Transcript excerpt: "At dawn, Naliaka and her brother carried seedlings to the school garden. Their teacher reminded the club to water the young trees before the sun became hot. By Friday, every group had labelled its row and recorded the number of seedlings planted."

Question: Which details should a listener record?

Model answer:
1. Who: Naliaka, her brother, the teacher, and the club.
2. Where: the school garden.
3. What happened: seedlings were planted, watered, labelled, and recorded.
4. Message: careful listening helps us retell events in order.

Grade 8 check: A strong listening answer names people, place, event order, and message without inventing details.

- Reading 2.2.1 Intensive: persona in a given poem

## Word classes: Collective Nouns.: Learn And Example

Begin with a real language task: a class discussion, reading club, letter, report, speech, or story situation linked to Word classes: Collective Nouns.

Important idea: Word classes: Collective Nouns.

Grammar helps meaning become clear. Do not memorise a rule alone; test it inside a real sentence. Check who or what the sentence is about, what action or state is shown, and how punctuation guides the reader.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

Success criteria:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

Inline visual support:

| Communication part | What to show |
| --- | --- |
| Purpose | The reading, listening, speaking, writing, or grammar task in Word classes: Collective Nouns. |
| Audience | Who needs to understand the message. |
| Evidence or example | A detail from the passage, conversation, notice, report, or spoken task. |
| Revision | One sentence improved for clarity, tone, spelling, punctuation, or grammar. |

Language model for Word classes: Collective Nouns.: Sort words by class before using them in sentences.

Word set: Nairobi, chair, water, courage, several, quickly.

Model classification:
1. Nairobi is a proper noun because it names a specific place.
2. Chair is a common count noun because we can count one chair, two chairs.
3. Water is a common non-count noun in ordinary use.
4. Courage is an abstract noun because it names a quality.
5. Several is a determiner because it tells about quantity.
6. Quickly is an adverb because it describes how an action is done.

Model sentence: Several learners quickly carried the chair from Nairobi Hall after the reading activity.

Explanation: Word classes help us choose correct forms and place words where the sentence makes sense.

Your turn: Classify four words linked to word classes: collective nouns., then use two of them in correct sentences.

Key words:

- Nouns.

Reading model for Reading 2.4.1 Intensive: Read the short passage, then answer with evidence.

Passage: Amina borrowed a library book about a school clean-water campaign. She first skimmed the headings, then read the middle paragraph slowly because it explained why learners labelled taps, reported leaks, and kept drinking cups clean. In her notebook, she wrote one main idea and two details from the paragraph instead of guessing from the title alone.

Question: What does Amina's behaviour show about careful reading?

Model answer:
1. Point: Amina reads carefully and uses evidence.
2. Evidence: She skims headings, rereads the paragraph, and records one main idea with two details.
3. Explanation: These actions show that a strong reading answer comes from the text, not from a quick guess.

Grade 8 check: A strong answer names the idea, quotes or paraphrases evidence, and explains why the evidence matters.

## Listening Comprehension: main idea from a cause and effect text: Learn And Example

Begin with a real language task: a class discussion, reading club, letter, report, speech, or story situation linked to Listening Comprehension: main idea from a cause and effect text.

Important idea: Listening Comprehension: main idea from a cause and effect text.

Reading well means tracking meaning, not just finishing words. Identify the main idea, notice how details support it, and answer with evidence from the text. For literature, connect character, setting, plot, language, and theme.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

For Grade 8, use clear sentences, examples, and respectful discussion.

Inline visual support:

| Communication part | What to show |
| --- | --- |
| Purpose | The reading, listening, speaking, writing, or grammar task in Listening Comprehension: main idea from a cause and effect text. |
| Audience | Who needs to understand the message. |

Listening model for Listening Comprehension: main idea from a cause and effect text: Catch key details from a recording or teacher-read text.

Transcript excerpt: "At dawn, Naliaka and her brother carried seedlings to the school garden. Their teacher reminded the club to water the young trees before the sun became hot. By Friday, every group had labelled its row and recorded the number of seedlings planted."

Question: Which details should a listener record?

Model answer:
1. Who: Naliaka, her brother, the teacher, and the club.
2. Where: the school garden.
3. What happened: seedlings were planted, watered, labelled, and recorded.
4. Message: careful listening helps us retell events in order.

Grade 8 check: A strong listening answer names people, place, event order, and message without inventing details.

## Reading 3.2.1 Intensive: Learn And Example

Before answering, decide whether you are reading, speaking, listening, writing, or editing.

Reading well means tracking meaning, not just finishing words. Identify the main idea, notice how details support it, and answer with evidence from the text. For literature, connect character, setting, plot, language, and theme.

| Communication part | What to show |
| --- | --- |
| Purpose | The reading, listening, speaking, writing, or grammar task in Reading 3.2.1 Intensive. |
| Audience | Who needs to understand the message. |
| Evidence or example | A detail from the passage, conversation, notice, report, or spoken task. |
| Revision | One sentence improved for clarity, tone, spelling, punctuation, or grammar. |

Reading model for Reading 3.2.1 Intensive: Read the short passage, then answer with evidence.

Passage: Amina borrowed a library book about a school clean-water campaign. She first skimmed the headings, then read the middle paragraph slowly because it explained why learners labelled taps, reported leaks, and kept drinking cups clean. In her notebook, she wrote one main idea and two details from the paragraph instead of guessing from the title alone.

Question: What does Amina's behaviour show about careful reading?

Model answer:
1. Point: Amina reads carefully and uses evidence.
2. Evidence: She skims headings, rereads the paragraph, and records one main idea with two details.
3. Explanation: These actions show that a strong reading answer comes from the text, not from a quick guess.

Grade 8 check: A strong answer names the idea, quotes or paraphrases evidence, and explains why the evidence matters.

Key words:
- Reading 3.2.1 Intensive
- Reading
- Intensive

## Writing 3.5.1: Learn And Example

Before answering, decide whether you are reading, speaking, listening, writing, or editing.

Important idea: Writing 3.5.1.

Writing improves when purpose, audience, structure, and editing work together. Plan the main idea first, arrange details in a sensible order, choose exact words, and revise at least one sentence after feedback.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

Success criteria:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

| Revision | One sentence improved for clarity, tone, spelling, punctuation, or grammar. |

Writing model for Writing 3.5.1: Plan before writing.

Task: Write a short response that explains a learning activity connected to this topic.

Plan:
1. Purpose: explain what happened and what was learned.
2. Audience: classmates, teacher, parents, or public readers.
3. Opening sentence: Our group studied writing 3.5.1 by reading the task, discussing evidence, and improving one draft.
4. Three details: what learners did, what evidence was used, and what changed after feedback.
5. Closing sentence: The activity showed that clear planning and revision improve communication.

Model paragraph: Our group studied writing 3.5.1 by reading the task, discussing evidence, and improving one draft. Each learner contributed one sentence, then the group checked word choice and punctuation. We revised the weakest sentence so the message became clearer. The activity showed that clear planning and revision improve communication.

Your turn: Use the same plan for a paragraph, report, letter, or speech linked to this topic.

4. Message: careful listening helps us retell events in order.

Grade 8 check: A strong listening answer names people, place, event order, and message without inventing details.

## Reading 4.2.1 Intensive: Learn And Example

Before answering, decide whether you are reading, speaking, listening, writing, or editing.

Important idea: Reading 4.2.1 Intensive.

Reading well means tracking meaning, not just finishing words. Identify the main idea, notice how details support it, and answer with evidence from the text. For literature, connect character, setting, plot, language, and theme.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

Success criteria:
- meaning is clear

Reading model for Reading 4.2.1 Intensive: Read the short passage, then answer with evidence.

Passage: Amina borrowed a library book about a school clean-water campaign. She first skimmed the headings, then read the middle paragraph slowly because it explained why learners labelled taps, reported leaks, and kept drinking cups clean. In her notebook, she wrote one main idea and two details from the paragraph instead of guessing from the title alone.

Question: What does Amina's behaviour show about careful reading?

Model answer:
1. Point: Amina reads carefully and uses evidence.
2. Evidence: She skims headings, rereads the paragraph, and records one main idea with two details.
3. Explanation: These actions show that a strong reading answer comes from the text, not from a quick guess.

Grade 8 check: A strong answer names the idea, quotes or paraphrases evidence, and explains why the evidence matters.

## Verbs and Tense: verbs in the simple present and simple past: Learn And Example

Begin with a real language task: a class discussion, reading club, letter, report, speech, or story situation linked to Verbs and Tense: verbs in the simple present and simple past.

Important idea: Verbs and Tense: verbs in the simple present and simple past.

Grammar helps meaning become clear. Do not memorise a rule alone; test it inside a real sentence. Check who or what the sentence is about, what action or state is shown, and how punctuation guides the reader.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

Success criteria:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

For Grade 8, use clear sentences, examples, and respectful discussion.

Inline visual support:

| Communication part | What to show |
| --- | --- |
| Purpose | The reading, listening, speaking, writing, or grammar task in Verbs and Tense: verbs in the simple present and simple past. |
| Audience | Who needs to understand the message. |
| Evidence or example | A detail from the passage, conversation, notice, report, or spoken task. |
| Revision | One sentence improved for clarity, tone, spelling, punctuation, or grammar. |

Language model for Verbs and Tense: verbs in the simple present and simple past: Make the verb agree with the subject and time.

Original sentence: The reading group discuss verbs and tense: verbs in the simple present and simple past yesterday and writes notes today.

Revision steps:
1. Find the subject: the reading group.
2. Match the time words: yesterday needs past tense; today needs present tense.
3. Keep each verb in the correct form.
4. Improved sentence: The reading group discussed verbs and tense: verbs in the simple present and simple past yesterday and writes notes today.

Explanation: Verb tense shows time, and agreement shows whether the subject is singular or plural.

Your turn: Write two correct sentences about verbs and tense: verbs in the simple present and simple past: one in the past tense and one in the present tense.

Key words:
- Verbs and Tense: verbs in the simple present and simple past
- Verbs and Tense

- Tense
- simple
- present

Writing improves when purpose, audience, structure, and editing work together. Plan the main idea first, arrange details in a sensible order, choose exact words, and revise at least one sentence after feedback.

Writing model for Writing 4.5.1 Paragraphing: Plan before writing.

Task: Write a short response that explains a learning activity connected to this topic.

Plan:
1. Purpose: explain what happened and what was learned.
2. Audience: classmates, teacher, parents, or public readers.
3. Opening sentence: Our group studied writing 4.5.1 paragraphing by reading the task, discussing evidence, and improving one draft.
4. Three details: what learners did, what evidence was used, and what changed after feedback.
5. Closing sentence: The activity showed that clear planning and revision improve communication.

Model paragraph: Our group studied writing 4.5.1 paragraphing by reading the task, discussing evidence, and improving one draft. Each learner contributed one sentence, then the group checked word choice and punctuation. We revised the weakest sentence so the message became clearer. The activity showed that clear planning and revision improve communication.

She first skimmed the headings, then read the middle paragraph slowly because it explained why learners labelled taps, reported leaks, and kept drinking cups clean. In her notebook, she wrote one main idea and two details from the paragraph instead of guessing from the title alone.

Question: What does Amina's behaviour show about careful reading?

Model answer:
1. Point: Amina reads carefully and uses evidence.
2. Evidence: She skims headings, rereads the paragraph, and records one main idea with two details.
3. Explanation: These actions show that a strong reading answer comes from the text, not from a quick guess.

Grade 8 check: A strong answer names the idea, quotes or paraphrases evidence, and explains why the evidence matters.

Key words:
- Reading 5.2.1 Study
- Reading
- Study

## Word Classes: adjectives: Learn And Example

Think of the listener or reader first. The task is connected to Word Classes: adjectives.

Important idea: Word Classes: adjectives.

Grammar helps meaning become clear. Do not memorise a rule alone; test it inside a real sentence. Check who or what the sentence is about, what action or state is shown, and how punctuation guides the reader.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

Success criteria:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

For Grade 8, use clear sentences, examples, and respectful discussion.

Inline visual support:

| Communication part | What to show |
| --- | --- |
| Purpose | The reading, listening, speaking, writing, or grammar task in Word Classes: adjectives. |
| Audience | Who needs to understand the message. |
| Evidence or example | A detail from the passage, conversation, notice, report, or spoken task. |
| Revision | One sentence improved for clarity, tone, spelling, punctuation, or grammar. |

Language model for Word Classes: adjectives: Sort words by class before using them in sentences.

Word set: Nairobi, chair, water, courage, several, quickly.

Model classification:
1. Nairobi is a proper noun because it names a specific place.
2. Chair is a common count noun because we can count one chair, two chairs.
3. Water is a common non-count noun in ordinary use.
4. Courage is an abstract noun because it names a quality.
5. Several is a determiner because it tells about quantity.
6. Quickly is an adverb because it describes how an action is done.

Model sentence: Several learners quickly carried the chair from Nairobi Hall after the reading activity.

Explanation: Word classes help us choose correct forms and place words where the sentence makes sense.

Your turn: Classify four words linked to word classes: adjectives, then use two of them in correct sentences.

Key words:
- Word Classes: adjectives

## Conversational Skills: Disagreeing politely: list ways of disagreeing politely: Learn And Example

Think of the listener or reader first. The task is connected to Conversational Skills: Disagreeing politely: list ways of disagreeing politely.

Important idea: Conversational Skills: Disagreeing politely: list ways of disagreeing politely.

Speaking and listening are active skills. Listen for the main point, take brief notes, respond respectfully, and support your own point with a reason or example. Clear speech uses pace, volume, eye contact, and appropriate tone.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

Success criteria:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

For Grade 8, use clear sentences, examples, and respectful discussion.

Inline visual support:

| Communication part | What to show |
| --- | --- |
| Purpose | The reading, listening, speaking, writing, or grammar task in Conversational Skills: Disagreeing politely: list ways of disagreeing politely. |
| Audience | Who needs to understand the message. |
| Evidence or example | A detail from the passage, conversation, notice, report, or spoken task. |
| Revision | One sentence improved for clarity, tone, spelling, punctuation, or grammar. |

Polite language model for Conversational Skills: Disagreeing politely: list ways of disagreeing politely: Match words to the setting and listener.

Situation: A learner is helping visitors during a school hospitality day. One visitor wants to reserve a table near a quiet corner.

Weak response: Sit there. I do not know if the table is free.

Improved response: Good afternoon. Let me check whether the quiet-corner table is available. If it has already been reserved, I can offer you another table near the window.

Why it works:
1. It opens politely.
2. It explains the action instead of sounding dismissive.
3. It gives an alternative.
4. It fits a hotel, dining, or public-service setting.

Your turn: Write a polite request, apology, or reservation response linked to conversational skills: disagreeing politely: list ways of disagreeing politely, then underline the words that make the tone respectful.

Key words:
- Conversational Skills: Disagreeing politely: list ways of disagreeing politely

## Word Classes: Adverbs: Learn And Example

Before answering, decide whether you are reading, speaking, listening, writing, or editing.

Important idea: Word Classes: Adverbs.

Grammar helps meaning become clear. Do not memorise a rule alone; test it inside a real sentence. Check who or what the sentence is about, what action or state is shown, and how punctuation guides the reader.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

Success criteria:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

For Grade 8, use clear sentences, examples, and respectful discussion.

Inline visual support:

| Communication part | What to show |
| --- | --- |
| Purpose | The reading, listening, speaking, writing, or grammar task in Word Classes: Adverbs. |
| Audience | Who needs to understand the message. |
| Evidence or example | A detail from the passage, conversation, notice, report, or spoken task. |
| Revision | One sentence improved for clarity, tone, spelling, punctuation, or grammar. |

Language model for Word Classes: Adverbs: Make the verb agree with the subject and time.

Original sentence: The reading group discuss word classes: adverbs yesterday and writes notes today.

Revision steps:
1. Find the subject: the reading group.
2. Match the time words: yesterday needs past tense; today needs present tense.
3. Keep each verb in the correct form.
4. Improved sentence: The reading group discussed word classes: adverbs yesterday and writes notes today.

Explanation: Verb tense shows time, and agreement shows whether the subject is singular or plural.

Your turn: Write two correct sentences about word classes: adverbs: one in the past tense and one in the present tense.

Key words:
- Word Classes: Adverbs
- Classes
- Adverbs

## Reading 6.4.1 Intensive: main ideas in the short story: Learn And Example

Begin with a real language task: a class discussion, reading club, letter, report, speech, or story situation linked to Reading 6.4.1 Intensive: main ideas in the short story.

Important idea: Reading 6.4.1 Intensive: main ideas in the short story.

Reading well means tracking meaning, not just finishing words. Identify the main idea, notice how details support it, and answer with evidence from the text. For literature, connect character, setting, plot, language, and theme.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

Success criteria:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

For Grade 8, use clear sentences, examples, and respectful discussion.

Inline visual support:

| Communication part | What to show |
| --- | --- |
| Purpose | The reading, listening, speaking, writing, or grammar task in Reading 6.4.1 Intensive: main ideas in the short story. |
| Audience | Who needs to understand the message. |
| Evidence or example | A detail from the passage, conversation, notice, report, or spoken task. |
| Revision | One sentence improved for clarity, tone, spelling, punctuation, or grammar. |

Reading model for Reading 6.4.1 Intensive: main ideas in the short story: Read the short passage, then answer with evidence.

Passage: Amina borrowed a library book about a school clean-water campaign. She first skimmed the headings, then read the middle paragraph slowly because it explained why learners labelled taps, reported leaks, and kept drinking cups clean. In her notebook, she wrote one main idea and two details from the paragraph instead of guessing from the title alone.

Question: What does Amina's behaviour show about careful reading?

Model answer:
1. Point: Amina reads carefully and uses evidence.
2. Evidence: She skims headings, rereads the paragraph, and records one main idea with two details.
3. Explanation: These actions show that a strong reading answer comes from the text, not from a quick guess.

Grade 8 check: A strong answer names the idea, quotes or paraphrases evidence, and explains why the evidence matters.

Key words:
- Reading 6.4.1 Intensive: main ideas in the short story

## Listening for Detail: Lesson Opener

Learning area: Listening for Detail

Start here: a class discussion, reading club, letter, report, speech, or story situation linked to Listening for Detail.

Curriculum link: This lesson follows the Grade 8 curriculum sequence and focuses on Listening for Detail.

By the end of this lesson sequence, you should be able to:
- identify main ideas in a listening text
- respond appropriately to questions based on the listening text
- acknowledge the importance of listening for details in life.

Inquiry questions:
- How should one listen for detail?

Before reading, write one thing you already know and one question you want this lesson to answer.

## Listening for Detail: Learn And Example

Think of the listener or reader first. The task is connected to Listening for Detail.

Important idea: Listening for Detail.

Speaking and listening are active skills. Listen for the main point, take brief notes, respond respectfully, and support your own point with a reason or example. Clear speech uses pace, volume, eye contact, and appropriate tone.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

Success criteria:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

For Grade 8, use clear sentences, examples, and respectful discussion.

Inline visual support:

| Communication part | What to show |
| --- | --- |
| Purpose | The reading, listening, speaking, writing, or grammar task in Listening for Detail. |
| Audience | Who needs to understand the message. |
| Evidence or example | A detail from the passage, conversation, notice, report, or spoken task. |
| Revision | One sentence improved for clarity, tone, spelling, punctuation, or grammar. |

Listening model for Listening for Detail: Catch key details from a recording or teacher-read text.

Transcript excerpt: "At dawn, Naliaka and her brother carried seedlings to the school garden. Their teacher reminded the club to water the young trees before the sun became hot. By Friday, every group had labelled its row and recorded the number of seedlings planted."

Question: Which details should a listener record?

Model answer:
1. Who: Naliaka, her brother, the teacher, and the club.
2. Where: the school garden.
3. What happened: seedlings were planted, watered, labelled, and recorded.
4. Message: careful listening helps us retell events in order.

Grade 8 check: A strong listening answer names people, place, event order, and message without inventing details.

Key words:
- Listening for Detail
- Listening
- Detail

Question: What does Amina's behaviour show about careful reading?

1. Point: Amina reads carefully and uses evidence.
2. Evidence: She skims headings, rereads the paragraph, and records one main idea with two details.

- use indefinite and reflexive pronouns in sentences correctly
- appreciate the role of indefinite and reflexive pronouns in communication.

Inquiry questions:

Before reading, write one thing you already know and one question you want this lesson to answer.

## Word Classes: Pronouns: Learn And Example

Think of the listener or reader first. The task is connected to Word Classes: Pronouns.

Important idea: Word Classes: Pronouns.

Grammar helps meaning become clear. Do not memorise a rule alone; test it inside a real sentence. Check who or what the sentence is about, what action or state is shown, and how punctuation guides the reader.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

Success criteria:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

For Grade 8, use clear sentences, examples, and respectful discussion.

Inline visual support:

| Communication part | What to show |
| --- | --- |
| Purpose | The reading, listening, speaking, writing, or grammar task in Word Classes: Pronouns. |
| Audience | Who needs to understand the message. |
| Evidence or example | A detail from the passage, conversation, notice, report, or spoken task. |
| Revision | One sentence improved for clarity, tone, spelling, punctuation, or grammar. |

Language model for Word Classes: Pronouns: Sort words by class before using them in sentences.

Word set: Nairobi, chair, water, courage, several, quickly.

Model classification:
1. Nairobi is a proper noun because it names a specific place.
2. Chair is a common count noun because we can count one chair, two chairs.
3. Water is a common non-count noun in ordinary use.
4. Courage is an abstract noun because it names a quality.
5. Several is a determiner because it tells about quantity.
6. Quickly is an adverb because it describes how an action is done.

Model sentence: Several learners quickly carried the chair from Nairobi Hall after the reading activity.

Explanation: Word classes help us choose correct forms and place words where the sentence makes sense.

Your turn: Classify four words linked to word classes: pronouns, then use two of them in correct sentences.

Key words:
- Word Classes: Pronouns

- Pronouns

Writing model for Writing 7.5.1 Composition: Plan before writing.

Task: Write a short response that explains a learning activity connected to this topic.

Plan:
1. Purpose: explain what happened and what was learned.
2. Audience: classmates, teacher, parents, or public readers.
3. Opening sentence: Our group studied writing 7.5.1 composition by reading the task, discussing evidence, and improving one draft.
4. Three details: what learners did, what evidence was used, and what changed after feedback.
5. Closing sentence: The activity showed that clear planning and revision improve communication.

Model paragraph: Our group studied writing 7.5.1 composition by reading the task, discussing evidence, and improving one draft. Each learner contributed one sentence, then the group checked word choice and punctuation. We revised the weakest sentence so the message became clearer. The activity showed that clear planning and revision improve communication.

Your turn: Use the same plan for a paragraph, report, letter, or speech linked to this topic.

## Listening and Responding: Oral Narratives - Myths: characters in a given myth: Learn And Example

Think of the listener or reader first. The task is connected to Listening and Responding: Oral Narratives - Myths: characters in a given myth.

Important idea: Listening and Responding: Oral Narratives - Myths: characters in a given myth.

Speaking and listening are active skills. Listen for the main point, take brief notes, respond respectfully, and support your own point with a reason or example. Clear speech uses pace, volume, eye contact, and appropriate tone.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

Success criteria:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

For Grade 8, use clear sentences, examples, and respectful discussion.

Inline visual support:

| Communication part | What to show |
| --- | --- |
| Purpose | The reading, listening, speaking, writing, or grammar task in Listening and Responding: Oral Narratives - Myths: characters in a given myth. |
| Audience | Who needs to understand the message. |
| Evidence or example | A detail from the passage, conversation, notice, report, or spoken task. |
| Revision | One sentence improved for clarity, tone, spelling, punctuation, or grammar. |

Listening model for Listening and Responding: Oral Narratives - Myths: characters in a given myth: Catch key details from a recording or teacher-read text.

Transcript excerpt: "At dawn, Naliaka and her brother carried seedlings to the school garden. Their teacher reminded the club to water the young trees before the sun became hot. By Friday, every group had labelled its row and recorded the number of seedlings planted."

Question: Which details should a listener record?

Model answer:
1. Who: Naliaka, her brother, the teacher, and the club.
2. Where: the school garden.
3. What happened: seedlings were planted, watered, labelled, and recorded.
4. Message: careful listening helps us retell events in order.

Grade 8 check: A strong listening answer names people, place, event order, and message without inventing details.

Key words:
- Listening and Responding: Oral Narratives - Myths: characters in a given myth

Passage: Amina borrowed a library book about a school clean-water campaign. She first skimmed the headings, then read the middle paragraph slowly because it explained why learners labelled taps, reported leaks, and kept drinking cups clean. Question: What does Amina's behaviour show about careful reading?

Model answer:
1. Point: Amina reads carefully and uses evidence.
2. Evidence: She skims headings, rereads the paragraph, and records one main idea with two details.
3. Explanation: These actions show that a strong reading answer comes from the text, not from a quick guess.

Reading model for Reading 10.4.1 Intensive: highlight the main events in the story: Read the short passage, then answer with evidence.

Passage: Amina borrowed a library book about a school clean-water campaign. She first skimmed the headings, then read the middle paragraph slowly because it explained why learners labelled taps, reported leaks, and kept drinking cups clean. In her notebook, she wrote one main idea and two details from the paragraph instead of guessing from the title alone.

Question: What does Amina's behaviour show about careful reading?

Model answer:
1. Point: Amina reads carefully and uses evidence.
2. Evidence: She skims headings, rereads the paragraph, and records one main idea with two details.
3. Explanation: These actions show that a strong reading answer comes from the text, not from a quick guess.

Grade 8 check: A strong answer names the idea, quotes or paraphrases evidence, and explains why the evidence matters.

## Conversational Skills: interviews: Learn And Example

Begin with a real language task: a class discussion, reading club, letter, report, speech, or story situation linked to Conversational Skills: interviews.

Important idea: Conversational Skills: interviews.

Speaking and listening are active skills. Listen for the main point, take brief notes, respond respectfully, and support your own point with a reason or example. Clear speech uses pace, volume, eye contact, and appropriate tone.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

Success criteria:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

For Grade 8, use clear sentences, examples, and respectful discussion.

| Communication part | What to show |
| --- | --- |
| Purpose | The reading, listening, speaking, writing, or grammar task in Conversational Skills: interviews. |
| Audience | Who needs to understand the message. |

| Revision | One sentence improved for clarity, tone, spelling, punctuation, or grammar. |

Oral language model for Conversational Skills: interviews: Prepare a clear contribution for discussion.

Topic: How can learners communicate this idea clearly and respectfully?

Model response:
1. Position: I think conversational skills: interviews needs clear examples and careful listening.
2. Reason: A listener understands better when the speaker gives one point at a time.
3. Evidence: In class, a speaker who names the topic, gives a reason, and pauses for questions is easier to follow.
4. Respectful close: I understand another group may explain it differently, but every answer should stay linked to evidence.

Speaking checklist: face the listener, speak at a steady pace, give one example, and respond politely to another person's point.

Your turn: Prepare a one-minute response connected to conversational skills: interviews.

## Word Classes: Adverbs: formed from adjectives in sentences: Learn And Example

Before answering, decide whether you are reading, speaking, listening, writing, or editing.

Important idea: Word Classes: Adverbs: formed from adjectives in sentences.

Grammar helps meaning become clear. Do not memorise a rule alone; test it inside a real sentence. Check who or what the sentence is about, what action or state is shown, and how punctuation guides the reader.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

Success criteria:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

For Grade 8, use clear sentences, examples, and respectful discussion.

Inline visual support:

| Communication part | What to show |
| --- | --- |
| Purpose | The reading, listening, speaking, writing, or grammar task in Word Classes: Adverbs: formed from adjectives in sentences. |
| Audience | Who needs to understand the message. |
| Evidence or example | A detail from the passage, conversation, notice, report, or spoken task. |
| Revision | One sentence improved for clarity, tone, spelling, punctuation, or grammar. |

Language model for Word Classes: Adverbs: formed from adjectives in sentences: Make the verb agree with the subject and time.

Original sentence: The reading group discuss word classes: adverbs: formed from adjectives in sentences yesterday and writes notes today.

Revision steps:
1. Find the subject: the reading group.
2. Match the time words: yesterday needs past tense; today needs present tense.
3. Keep each verb in the correct form.
4. Improved sentence: The reading group discussed word classes: adverbs: formed from adjectives in sentences yesterday and writes notes today.

Explanation: Verb tense shows time, and agreement shows whether the subject is singular or plural.

Your turn: Write two correct sentences about word classes: adverbs: formed from adjectives in sentences: one in the past tense and one in the present tense.

Reading model for Reading 11.4.1 Intensive: Read the short passage, then answer with evidence.

Passage: Amina borrowed a library book about a school clean-water campaign. She first skimmed the headings, then read the middle paragraph slowly because it explained why learners labelled taps, reported leaks, and kept drinking cups clean. In her notebook, she wrote one main idea and two details from the paragraph instead of guessing from the title alone.

Question: What does Amina's behaviour show about careful reading?

Model answer:
1. Point: Amina reads carefully and uses evidence.
2. Evidence: She skims headings, rereads the paragraph, and records one main idea with two details.
3. Explanation: These actions show that a strong reading answer comes from the text, not from a quick guess.

Grade 8 check: A strong answer names the idea, quotes or paraphrases evidence, and explains why the evidence matters.

## Writing 11.5.1 Mechanics: Learn And Example

Before answering, decide whether you are reading, speaking, listening, writing, or editing.

Important idea: Writing 11.5.1 Mechanics.

Writing improves when purpose, audience, structure, and editing work together. Plan the main idea first, arrange details in a sensible order, choose exact words, and revise at least one sentence after feedback.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

Success criteria:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

| Revision | One sentence improved for clarity, tone, spelling, punctuation, or grammar. |

Writing model for Writing 11.5.1 Mechanics: Plan before writing.

Task: Write a short response that explains a learning activity connected to this topic.

Plan:
1. Purpose: explain what happened and what was learned.
2. Audience: classmates, teacher, parents, or public readers.
3. Opening sentence: Our group studied writing 11.5.1 mechanics by reading the task, discussing evidence, and improving one draft.
4. Three details: what learners did, what evidence was used, and what changed after feedback.
5. Closing sentence: The activity showed that clear planning and revision improve communication.

Model paragraph: Our group studied writing 11.5.1 mechanics by reading the task, discussing evidence, and improving one draft. Each learner contributed one sentence, then the group checked word choice and punctuation. We revised the weakest sentence so the message became clearer. The activity showed that clear planning and revision improve communication.

Your turn: Use the same plan for a paragraph, report, letter, or speech linked to this topic.

| Revision | One sentence improved for clarity, tone, spelling, punctuation, or grammar. |

Listening model for Listening to Respond: Attitude: Catch key details from a recording or teacher-read text.

Transcript excerpt: "At dawn, Naliaka and her brother carried seedlings to the school garden. Their teacher reminded the club to water the young trees before the sun became hot. By Friday, every group had labelled its row and recorded the number of seedlings planted."

Question: Which details should a listener record?

Model answer:
1. Who: Naliaka, her brother, the teacher, and the club.
2. Where: the school garden.
3. What happened: seedlings were planted, watered, labelled, and recorded.
4. Message: careful listening helps us retell events in order.

Grade 8 check: A strong listening answer names people, place, event order, and message without inventing details.

Key words:
- Listening to Respond: Attitude

- Respond

Listening model for Extensive Listening: Poems: Catch key details from a recording or teacher-read text.

Transcript excerpt: "At dawn, Naliaka and her brother carried seedlings to the school garden. Their teacher reminded the club to water the young trees before the sun became hot. By Friday, every group had labelled its row and recorded the number of seedlings planted."

Question: Which details should a listener record?

Model answer:
1. Who: Naliaka, her brother, the teacher, and the club.
2. Where: the school garden.
3. What happened: seedlings were planted, watered, labelled, and recorded.
4. Message: careful listening helps us retell events in order.

Grade 8 check: A strong listening answer names people, place, event order, and message without inventing details.

Passage: Amina borrowed a library book about a school clean-water campaign. Question: What does Amina's behaviour show about careful reading?

Model answer:
1. Point: Amina reads carefully and uses evidence.
2. Evidence: She skims headings, rereads the paragraph, and records one main idea with two details.
3. Explanation: These actions show that a strong reading answer comes from the text, not from a quick guess.

## Word Classes: Compound Sentences: Learn And Example

Before answering, decide whether you are reading, speaking, listening, writing, or editing.

Important idea: Word Classes: Compound Sentences.

Grammar helps meaning become clear. Do not memorise a rule alone; test it inside a real sentence. Check who or what the sentence is about, what action or state is shown, and how punctuation guides the reader.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

| Revision | One sentence improved for clarity, tone, spelling, punctuation, or grammar. |

Language model for Word Classes: Compound Sentences: Join ideas without making the sentence confusing.

Original sentence: The class read the passage. The passage explained word classes: compound sentences. The group needed evidence.

Revision steps:
1. Keep the main idea clear.
2. Join related ideas with because, when, although, or which only where the meaning fits.
3. Remove repeated words.
4. Improved sentence: When the class read the passage about word classes: compound sentences, the group underlined evidence before answering.

Explanation: A useful complex sentence links ideas and still keeps the main action easy to find.

Your turn: Combine two short sentences about word classes: compound sentences into one clear sentence.

Reading model for Reading 14.2.1 Study: main ideas in a descriptive text: Read the short passage, then answer with evidence.

Passage: Amina borrowed a library book about a school clean-water campaign. She first skimmed the headings, then read the middle paragraph slowly because it explained why learners labelled taps, reported leaks, and kept drinking cups clean. In her notebook, she wrote one main idea and two details from the paragraph instead of guessing from the title alone.

Question: What does Amina's behaviour show about careful reading?

Model answer:
1. Point: Amina reads carefully and uses evidence.
2. Evidence: She skims headings, rereads the paragraph, and records one main idea with two details.
3. Explanation: These actions show that a strong reading answer comes from the text, not from a quick guess.

Grade 8 check: A strong answer names the idea, quotes or paraphrases evidence, and explains why the evidence matters.

## Sentences: Active and Passive Voice: sentences in the active and passive voice: Learn And Example

Begin with a real language task: a class discussion, reading club, letter, report, speech, or story situation linked to Sentences: Active and Passive Voice: sentences in the active and passive voice.

Important idea: Sentences: Active and Passive Voice: sentences in the active and passive voice.

Grammar helps meaning become clear. Do not memorise a rule alone; test it inside a real sentence. Check who or what the sentence is about, what action or state is shown, and how punctuation guides the reader.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

Success criteria:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

For Grade 8, use clear sentences, examples, and respectful discussion.

Inline visual support:

| Communication part | What to show |
| --- | --- |
| Purpose | The reading, listening, speaking, writing, or grammar task in Sentences: Active and Passive Voice: sentences in the active and passive voice. |
| Audience | Who needs to understand the message. |
| Evidence or example | A detail from the passage, conversation, notice, report, or spoken task. |
| Revision | One sentence improved for clarity, tone, spelling, punctuation, or grammar. |

Language model for Sentences: Active and Passive Voice: sentences in the active and passive voice: Improve a sentence so the meaning is precise.

Draft sentence: The learner wrote clear examples, but the explanation need one reason.

Revision steps:
1. Find the subject of the second clause: the explanation.
2. Check the verb form: explanation is singular, so use needs.
3. Keep the correction small so the meaning stays the same.
4. Improved sentence: The learner wrote clear examples, but the explanation needs one reason.

Explanation: Correct agreement and exact word order help the reader understand the idea without guessing.

Your turn: Write one correct sentence about sentences: active and passive voice: sentences in the active and passive voice, then underline the subject and verb.

2. Evidence: She skims headings, rereads the paragraph, and records one main idea with two details.
3. Explanation: These actions show that a strong reading answer comes from the text, not from a quick guess.

Grade 8 check: A strong answer names the idea, quotes or paraphrases evidence, and explains why the evidence matters.

Listening model for Reading 15.2.1 Reading: Recorded Stories, Poems, and Dialogues: Catch key details from a recording or teacher-read text.

Transcript excerpt: "At dawn, Naliaka and her brother carried seedlings to the school garden. Their teacher reminded the club to water the young trees before the sun became hot. By Friday, every group had labelled its row and recorded the number of seedlings planted."

Question: Which details should a listener record?

Model answer:
1. Who: Naliaka, her brother, the teacher, and the club.
2. Where: the school garden.
3. What happened: seedlings were planted, watered, labelled, and recorded.
4. Message: careful listening helps us retell events in order.

Grade 8 check: A strong listening answer names people, place, event order, and message without inventing details.

## Answer And Teacher Notes

A strong English answer should:

- match the purpose and audience;
- use evidence from the passage, discussion, task, or real context;
- organise ideas into clear sentences and paragraphs;
- choose words that fit the tone;
- edit spelling, punctuation, grammar, and meaning after feedback.

For reading questions, use point, evidence, and explanation. For writing tasks, plan before writing and revise at least one sentence before submitting.

Answer-key guide: reading answers need a point, a quoted or paraphrased detail, and an explanation. Grammar answers need the corrected sentence and the reason for the correction. Writing answers need purpose, audience, structure, and editing evidence. Oral tasks need a clear position, reason, example, and respectful close.

Rubric for self-checking:
- 4: The answer addresses the exact question, uses subject vocabulary correctly, gives evidence or working, and includes a useful check or correction.
- 3: The main answer is correct but one part needs stronger evidence, clearer working, better labels, or a fuller explanation.
- 2: Some understanding is shown, but the answer is incomplete, too general, or hard to follow.
- 1: The answer is guessed, off topic, or missing the evidence needed for the task.

Correction routine: reread the command word, underline the key data or evidence, improve one weak step, and write one sentence explaining why the final answer is reasonable.

## Final Project

Create a final project for English.

Your project must include:
1. A clear title.
2. A real-life problem or question.
3. Evidence from at least three lessons.
4. A drawing, table, map, chart, model, dialogue, performance plan, artwork note, or worked solution.
5. A short reflection explaining what you learned.

Present your project to a classmate, teacher, parent, or study group.
